## assistant @ 2026-09-24T02:10:40.412Z

MoJev is worth a close look, but it won't do what you described out of the box. Two facts change the plan.

**1. It doesn't write summaries.** MoJev is a scorer, like Laya. You give it some text, a question and a list of allowed answers. It returns a probability for each answer. It can't write a summary or a free-text analysis, so summaries still need a model that generates text, like the local Qwen.

**2. The 1M context is headroom, not proven quality.** It was trained on inputs of up to 16,384 tokens, and it uses that 16k window by default.
- The 262k figure comes from its base model, Qwen3.5-0.8B.
- The 1M figure needs a YaRN setting that the released model doesn't turn on.
- The README reports no accuracy above 16k. The audit is checking the paper for one.

**Where it could fit your idea.** It can answer typed questions over a long input in one pass. That suits a session-level check between compactions, for example:
- "Is work left unpushed?" (yes/no)
- "Which lane is blocked?" (pick one)
- "Did this report skip a required gate?" (a score)

That would be an advisory signal only, never a gate. So the split works: Laya handles short one-off judgments (512–1024 tokens), and MoJev handles bigger inputs. It is at least 16x Laya's window where it was actually trained.

**Risks I can already see:**
- **It runs downloaded code.** At startup it runs custom code from Hugging Face. We would pin the exact version and read that code before running it.
- **It is one day old.** One commit and 14 downloads. Its 93% accuracy claim is on its own dataset. Laya claimed 77%, and we measured 36% on our data. We test MoJev on our data before trusting it.
- **It may be slow on our PC.** The GPU is full with the Qwen server, and very long inputs on the CPU will be slow. How slow needs a measurement, not a guess.
- **The never-a-gate screen won't catch it.** The screen that keeps these models out of gates doesn't recognise the name "mojev" yet. It must learn the name before any adoption.

**What I started:** a read-only audit. One agent is reading the code, the paper and the Hugging Face model page, with no install and no network access. It will also measure how big our sessions actually get between compactions. When it reports back, I'll write up the findings with options for you to choose from. Nothing is installed.

Also still running: J1-3-R1 and K150 in the sandbox, J1-1-R3 on the PC, and the CI run.

## assistant @ 2026-09-24T02:17:55.985Z

All three agents are running again, on Opus 5.5 this time.

- **Opus 5 fixed:** the audit ran on Opus 5 because that is the default pinned in the evidence agent's settings file. I've recorded your correction as decision D-065: every exploration agent now runs on Opus 5.5, like the build and verify agents.
- **Little work lost:** K150 kept the three items it had finished. J1-3-R1 had only done its premise check. The audit hadn't written anything yet, so it starts fresh with your new ideas added.

Your three new ideas are now questions 11 to 13 in the audit. My first take on each:

1. **Bug finding: a good fit.** The code tools list the candidate functions (the callers and the call chain). MoJev reads their outputs plus the error trace and ranks which function is most likely at fault. That tells us where to look first; we still reproduce the bug before believing it. We can test it on our own history, since we have many past bugs where we know which function the fix changed.

2. **Workflow reminders: a good fit if it adds reminders rather than removing them.** It can add targeted ones, such as "this batch fixed a bug class that isn't in the registry yet." Letting it silently skip the current reminder is a different matter. A model would then be deciding a gate, which the rule we set for Laya forbids. You can change that rule. If you want to, I'd run MoJev beside the current hook for a week first and compare. The audit also counts how often that reminder fires and how big it is, so we'll know what it costs today.

3. **Context manager: the strongest fit of the three.** It is the relevance filter we already planned for Laya, but with a much bigger window. One correction to the split: MoJev can't search. It only scores options it is given. So:
   - our existing tools (graft, the wiki, the ledger) find the candidate files and snippets;
   - Laya can do the quick "what should we look for" step;
   - MoJev ranks the candidates against the current task;
   - the hook injects the top few, within a size budget.

   The core context (CLAUDE.md, the live-state page) stays injected every time, whatever the model says.

In all three roles MoJev only advises; nothing it says can pass or fail a check. When the audit reports back, I'll write up the findings with options for you to choose from.

We're about **28% of the way through the whole plan**, measured by effort. How you weigh the stages moves that between 24% and 31%, so treat it as a quarter to a third.

The plan in `docs/07_BUILD_PLAN.md` has nine stages, 0 to 8. The first stage, proving the pieces work together before building on them, is nearly done. One later stage is about two-thirds built. The other seven have a design decision at most, and no production code yet.

**How I got the number:** I scored each stage from the repo's own records, then weighted it by my estimate of its share of the total effort. The weights are my judgment, which is why I give a range.

| Stage | What it delivers | Where it stands | Done | Weight |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0 Proof pack | Evidence that each piece works | All 12 proofs pass on real runs. You signed 4 (S0-01, S0-02, S0-05, S0-11); 8 wait for your review | ~95% | 20 |
| 1 Production spine | Buzz → buzz-acp → Hermes → OmniRoute as a running, pinned service | Works in proof runs on your PC; nothing deployed | ~10% | 15 |
| 2 Four-scope memory | ai-memory plus our memory provider | Design proven; provider not built | ~10% | 12 |
| 3 Governance | Fubuki rules, hash, read-only rules copy for Hermes | Core built and independently verified; wiring into sessions waits on Stage 1 | ~65% | 8 |
| 4 Security gate | Policy service, fail-closed tool hook, production sandboxing (gVisor), outbound-traffic control | Containment proven; none of the services built | ~5% | 15 |
| 5 Dream phase | Improvement proposals from GBrain | Design decision only | ~5% | 10 |
| 6 JIT Foundry | Generated agent-harness candidates | Design decision only | ~5% | 10 |
| 7 HarnessRouter | Only if a harness can't use ACP | Not started; may never be needed | 0% | 3 |
| 8 Operations | Load tests, backups, runbooks | Not started | 0% | 7 |

**What's done:**
- **The plan.** Architecture, component audit, council review, interview, seed and task breakdown.
- **All 12 Stage 0 proofs.** The spine proofs came from real runs on your PC: Buzz, buzz-acp, Hermes and OmniRoute talking to each other; authorization and replay rejection; no direct model traffic; gVisor containment.
- **The proof machinery.** Validator, runner, ledger, CI checks and your signed-tag sign-off.
- **The governance core** (Stage 3). Built and passed an independent adversarial check. There are about 2,000 lines of production Python so far.
- **The tooling.** PC build lanes on the local Qwen model, CI and push gates, the verify discipline, and an incident registry with 170 recorded anti-patterns.

**What's left, in order:**
1. **Close Stage 0:**
   - your review and signature on the 8 remaining proofs (S0-03, S0-04, S0-06, S0-07, S0-08, S0-09, S0-10, S0-12), one command each, like the last three;
   - then the Stage 0 pull request to `main`, which only you can merge.

## assistant @ 2026-09-24T03:32:55.578Z

**MoJev is worth one small, bounded test. It is not the million-token session reader it appears to be.** The full write-up is in `docs/research/findings/MOJEV-AUDIT-2026-09-24.md`. I'll push it when the current CI run finishes.

**What MoJev really is**
- **It picks; it does not write.** You give it text and some candidate answers, and it scores each candidate. It cannot produce a summary.
- **The 1-million figure belongs to the Qwen base model.** MoJev was trained and tested only up to 16,000 tokens. Longer text is cut off silently, with no warning.
- **A whole session is far too big for it.** One of our sessions reaches about 787,000 tokens before a compaction, which is 48 times its tested limit. At that size, one of its internal tables alone would need about 2.5 TB of memory.

**How it fits your four ideas**
- **Reading a whole session or Hermes lane at once:** no. It can only look at 16k-token slices, one narrow question at a time.
- **Finding the function behind a bug:** a good fit. "Here is the failing test and the error trace; which of these functions is at fault?" is exactly the kind of question it answers. We also have real answers to score it against: the function each fix commit changed.
- **Choosing what context to show you:** the best fit. Our existing search finds the candidates, and MoJev ranks them against the current task. It only adds context; it never hides any.
- **Knowing when to update the wiki, skills or anti-patterns:** a partial fit. The hard part is training data. If we learn from our own history, it learns our forgetting.
- Pairing it with Laya still makes sense: Laya for fast, short checks (512 tokens), MoJev for questions that need more text (up to 16,000 tokens).

**Blockers:** the GPU is full with the Qwen lanes, so MoJev would run on the PC's CPU. My estimate is about a minute per full-size question; I haven't measured it. Its bundled server isn't safe to run as it is, so we would put it behind our own.

**A surprise you should know about:** our own reminder and context hooks have not been running in this session. They only load when a session starts inside the agent-factory folder, and this one started one folder up. The "untracked files" reminder you see after most turns comes from Claude Code itself, not from our hooks. So the wiki reminder you thought was wasting tokens was actually switched off. I corrected CLAUDE.md, which said the hooks were running.

**Two decisions I need from you**
1. MoJev:
   - **A:** park it.
   - **B (my pick):** a bounded test on your PC's CPU, bug-finding first, scored against the real fix commits. This needs your OK to run MoJev's code and weights on your PC.
   - **C:** wait until someone publishes a measured long-context result.
2. Our five hooks. My pick is to restore all five, with the code check running only when files are edited, not every time one is read. I'll measure the token cost for a day and report back. Or tell me which ones you want.

**Status**
- [x] MoJev findings written, with options.
- [x] Five new bug patterns logged in the anti-pattern list, two older entries corrected, and issues #11 and #17 closed (S0-02 is accepted).
- [ ] Three sandbox agents are running: two checks of earlier work, and one fix for the redaction bugs.
- [ ] Six local commits are waiting for CI run #1015 to finish (runs take about 25 minutes), then I push.

```
cd ~/agent-factory && git fetch origin && git merge --ff-only origin/claude/soundbox-kit-migration-iz1jwf && for p in S0-03 S0-04 S0-06 S0-07 S0-08 S0-09 S0-10 S0-12; do git rev-parse -q --verify "refs/tags/accepted/$p" >/dev/null || git -c gpg.format=openpgp tag -s "accepted/$p" -m "ACCEPTED: $p - owner acceptance of the current minted result" HEAD || break; done; git tag -l 'accepted/*'
```

- **First it updates your PC copy.** I checked it just now: it's on the right branch and has no local changes. The update changes only three docs files, so it won't disturb the K170 lane running there.
- **Your key may ask for its passphrase.** Usually that happens only once for the whole batch.
- **If a step fails, it stops.** You can safely run it again, because tags that already exist are skipped.

Signing only needs you because the signature must come from your key. Any later re-sign will also be one command, and I'll give it to you ready to paste.

## 1. The redaction pass (task #198)
J1-1 removes secrets from a decision record before the record is stored. Three repair rounds each closed the leak they were aimed at, but each left or added another. The last independent verify reproduced two leaks.

- **A. Redesign (recommended).** Replace the stack of regexes with one scanning pass and one rule: once a secret's name is seen, remove everything up to the end of its value. There is one known trap. If the scan stops at the start of a value, the rest can leak (3.4 to 3.7% of random inputs). The new design removes such a value whole.
- **B. One more patch.** The verifier's trial fix passed 177 tests with no leak in 60,000 random inputs. But it's a scratch test, not a built and verified change. It also breaks our rule against a fourth patch (D-059).
- **C. Park J1-1.** Nothing reads the decision ledger yet, so nothing leaks today. The Laya ledger work stays on hold.

**Why A:** the leaks come from regexes interacting with each other. Each patch fixed only the case it was told about. One pass with one rule can be tested thoroughly. B's numbers look good, but so did the three earlier patches until a verify broke them. A costs one build lane and one verify.

## 2. MoJev (task #210)
MoJev doesn't read and summarize. It scores answers: you give it up to 16k tokens of text and some candidate answers, and it rates each one. Our sessions run to about 787k tokens, so it can't read a whole session.

- **A. Park it.** Fix the hooks (decision 3), and look again once someone publishes a tested long-context result.
- **B. A small, capped trial after the hook fix (recommended).** It runs on your PC's CPU, with no GPU. First I measure its speed and memory use. Then I test whether it can find the faulty function in 100 or more past bugs, where the real answer is the function the fix changed. If it doesn't beat two simple baselines, we stop.
- **C. Wait** for a published result.

**Why B:** it's cheap, and it has a hard stop. The fix commits are a real answer key, so it can't pass by accident. A and C cost nothing, but we learn nothing. B needs your yes to run MoJev's code and model files on your PC, because the sandbox refuses to run outside code.

## 3. Project hooks (task #214)
Five hooks exist but never fire, because this session starts in /home/user instead of the repo. For example, the end-of-turn review checklist ran 0 times in 834 turns.

- **Session start** (loads the current project state): about 550 tokens per start or context reset.
- **Wiki excerpts** added to each message you send: about 550 tokens per message.
- **End-of-turn review:** about 400 tokens, plus one extra turn each time a batch of work lands.

**Owner rulings in force:**
- D-060: save tokens.
- D-061: local Hermes only.

- D-065: pass `model: "opus"` on every dispatch.

**Owner-facing style:** answer first, short sentences, no em-dashes in chat, Rundown shape for status. The owner reliably sees only the FINAL message.

**PERSISTED SECURITY/OPERATIONAL CONSTRAINTS** (verbatim from prior summaries; still in effect):

*Credentials and secrets*
- Never print, paste or copy an OmniRoute/model API key or bridge token into chat, shell history or argv (AF-AP-39). Read keys in place.
- `.pc-bridge.env` is untracked and gitignored. Update it only through the Write tool, never through argv.
- A key NAME in a committed config is OK; a VALUE never is.
- Bridge reads of config files go through ONE redacting filter at the END of the pipeline.
- Never print `~/.hermes/profiles/agentfactory/.env` or any `*.env`.
- `TYPESAFE_API_KEY=local` is a placeholder.
- The sandbox env has GH_TOKEN/GITHUB_TOKEN-named vars; never print them.
- Print proxy/credential info as shape, host and port only.
- Test secrets are FAKE strings only (QZJ8…, X4Z9… style).
- The pair.env / nonmember.env / owner*.env secrets are never printed; only public halves are recorded.
- Mask the relay's DATABASE_URL password.

*The owner's servers and config*
- Never stop or restart the owner's servers: OmniRoute, the vLLM `qwen` container, `buzz-prod-*`, Ollama, Phoenix, the `laya-systemone` unit, the test relay :3999 (pid 53277), the scripted backend :20201 (pid 53658), and the rest.
- Never modify the owner's OmniRoute/Hermes config or `~/.hermes/profiles/agentfactory/`.
- The T93-R1 profile change is OWNER-RUN (`bash scripts/t93r1_apply_profile.sh`).
- Freeing GPU memory held by vLLM is the owner's decision.
- DB probes are read-only; writes are owner-run.
- Read-only GETs to the owner's services are OK.
- Read OmniRoute call_logs with a python sqlite3 `mode=ro` query shipped base64.

*Commits and pushes*
- Commit with `git reset -q; bash scripts/safe_commit.sh -m "$(cat msgfile)" <paths>`, or a single-quoted message with no backticks in double quotes.
- Push only via `git checkout -- AGENTS.md CLAUDE.md; git fetch -q origin <branch> && PUSH_BRANCH=claude/soundbox-ki

**Your three answers are in the decision log (D-072):**

**Five sandbox lanes are building now:**
- **JT1:** the Jev tool (`scripts/jev.py`) and the quirk tracker. The tool's live checks against the local Jev server passed. The tracker page is still being built.
- **JT2, the bug locator you described:** you give it a bug. It runs every code-intel tool (graft, GitNexus, codebase-memory, code-review-graph, rg, the anti-pattern registry, git history). Jev ranks what they found, and you get the files and lines to read, under a size cap.
  - It includes a check that it actually helps: 20 past fixes where we know which files were fixed. It measures how often those files land in the top 5, with and without Jev's ranking.
- **JT2, Jev-assisted bug-echo:** it takes a fix, finds the look-alike sites, and Jev scores them. The model then reads the top 10 sites, not every match. The bug-or-not call stays with the model.
- **JT3, the Grep intercept:** a semantic Grep, or a plain grep of project code, is blocked once. You get graft's answer plus the ranked hits. Repeating the same call within 2 minutes gets the raw result, so a wrong intercept costs one call.
  - The same hook blocks the command shapes that keep biting me, like the trailing `&` that bit again today. A shape ships only if a check against this session's history shows at most 5% false alarms.
- **MoJev Gate 0:** the probe is built here, and I run it on the PC's CPU, where the model files are.

**One finding you should know:** the output pruner plugin does nothing in this harness either. A 247 KB output reached the local Jev server 0 times. On your question, here is the full picture.

**Laya failed, but "a model can't do this" is not proven yet.** J2 tested one small open model (Laya, 421M parameters), asked one way, on two tasks. Three things could be at fault:
1. **How we asked.** We scored each candidate alone. A single multiple-choice question may work better.
2. **This model.** Laya is small and has a short input window (1,024 tokens).
3. **The task.** These may be hard for any small model.

**Running now, to tell those apart (results in about 30-60 minutes):**
- Laya re-asked three ways on the same samples: one multiple-choice question over the candidates, fuller class definitions, and a plain yes/no "is this blocking?".
- Haiku, a small general model, on the same 200 questions with the answers hidden. If Haiku does well, the task is learnable and Laya is the weak part.

**Then, to get a model that works:**
- **MoJev** is a bigger open model built for this kind of decision, with a 16k window. Its probe is almost built, and the PC slot is now free to run it.
- **The hosted Jev** (TypeSafe's own model) was never tested. In CLM's published test it matched the planner 98.7% of the time. It needs your TypeSafe API key, routed through OmniRoute (rule 3).
- **Fine-tuning** Laya or MoJev on our own labels. The J1 ledger now collects them. This needs GPU time, so it comes later.

**Do you have a TypeSafe key you'd like me to wire up through OmniRoute?** If the other paths fall short, the hosted model is the likely fix.

Until one of these wins, the tools keep their model hook switched off for ranking only. They flip to the model the moment it beats the plain order.

## assistant @ 2026-09-24T14:44:02.750Z

Yes, let's fine-tune Laya and keep it. On your CPU, training takes about 40 minutes if we train only its small decision head, or 5 to 14 hours for the whole model. Codiv's OpenJev works and can label the training data, but it needs one "yes" from you first (see "What I need from you").

**How long a fine-tune takes** (measured on your PC just now, 12 CPU threads, 8 examples per step):

| Input size | Whole model | Head only |
|---|---|---|
| 256 tokens | 24 s per step | 5 s per step |
| 512 tokens | 67 s per step | 23 s per step |

For 2,000 labeled examples:
- **Head only:** about 40 minutes. The big encoder runs once per example and its output is reused.
- **Whole model, 3 passes:** about 5 hours at 256 tokens, about 14 hours at 512.
- **On the 3090:** probably minutes (my estimate, not measured). But vLLM holds all but about 0.5 GB of it.

My plan is head-only first. If it beats the plain baselines on rows it never trained on, we stop there. If not, we train the whole model overnight.

**Laya in vLLM?** I wouldn't do it.
- vLLM runs one model per process and reserves its GPU memory up front, so two models can't share one KV cache.
- vLLM also can't run Laya's custom decision head.
- Laya doesn't need the GPU to answer anyway: on your CPU it takes about 0.3 s per question.
- The GPU only matters for training and for MoJev. Both would need 2 to 4 GB freed, which means restarting vLLM with less memory and stopping any running lane. That's your call.

**The 0.8B one (MoJev) on your CPU:** it runs, but too slowly for quick automatic decisions. I ran a small test just now (2,048 tokens, 5 options):
- **FP32:** 15.7 s per decision, using 5.8 GB of memory.
- **bf16:** 73 s per decision, because your CPU has no native bf16 support.
- **Laya:** about 0.3 s for comparison.

The full fit test (30 tasks) is running on your PC now and will take a few hours.

**Codiv (OpenJev 0.1):**
- The key works. A made-up test question came back correct in 1.6 s.
- OpenJev is a much bigger model than Laya (26B total, 4B active).
- The limit is 60 calls a minute. 2,000 labels take about 35 minutes and use roughly 1% of your free tokens.
- The key is stored outside the repo and never printed. Since it's in this chat now, rotate it once the free tokens run out.

**GEPA:** yes, it fits. It tunes each question's instructions and answer options using about 30 hard labeled examples. Their post reports about 43 points better on average across 11 models. We'd use it twice:
- Tune OpenJev's questions before it labels data. A better teacher gives a better Laya.
- Tune Laya's questions after training.

It needs a text model to suggest the edits, and your local Qwen can do that. It's third-party code, so I'll pin its version before using it.

**One lesson that shapes the training data:** we train on full text, not titles. The finding-class rows hold only a 120-character title, and neither Laya nor Haiku could classify from that.

This is what gemeni said 

When comparing [Layla AI](https://www.layla-network.ai/) (which runs local Transformer models like Llama 3.2 via GGUF format on your phone) with training or running an RWKV (Recurrent Neural Network) model, there are major differences in how they perform, especially for on-device or local use cases. [1, 2, 3, 4, 5] 
The benefits of training and running RWKV instead of a standard transformer setup are detailed below.
## 1. Zero KV Cache Growth (Massive RAM Savings)

* 
* The Problem with Layla/GGUF: Transformers require a "KV Cache" to remember context. As your chat with a character or assistant grows longer, the KV cache eats up more and more of your phone's RAM. Eventually, the app slows down or crashes. [2, 6] 
* The RWKV Benefit: RWKV processes text like an RNN. It condenses the entire conversation history into a hidden state vector of a fixed size. Whether your context is 500 tokens or 50,000 tokens, the memory footprint remains constant ($O(1)$ memory complexity). It will never bloat your phone's RAM, no matter how long the roleplay or document is. [4, 5, 6, 7, 8] 
* 

## 2. Blazing Fast Generation at Infinite Context

* 
* The Problem with Layla/GGUF: As context scales in a Transformer, generating each new token takes progressively longer because it must look back at every previous token (quadratic $O(N^2)$ complexity). [6] 
* The RWKV Benefit: Because it uses linear attention, generating a new token takes the exact same amount of time at token 1 as it does at token 10,000. [7, 8] 
* 

## 3. Substantially Lower Training & Fine-Tuning Costs
If you are planning to fine-tune a model on your own chat logs, custom data, or lorebooks: [9] 

* 
* Linear Training Scalability: During training, RWKV can behave like a Transformer, allowing for parallelised training across multiple documents. Because it scales linearly ($O(N)$) rather than quadratically with sequence length, you can fine-tune RWKV on massive context windows using a fraction of the VRAM that a standard Transformer would require. [6, 8, 10] 
* 

------------------------------
## Comparison Summary

| Feature | Layla (Standard Transformers / GGUF) | RWKV (RNN Architecture) |
|---|---|---|
| Memory Usage (RAM) | Increases the longer the chat gets | Stays completely flat ($O(1)$) |
| Generation Speed | Slows down at long contexts | Remains perfectly consistent |
| Long Context Limits | Restricted by hardware RAM limits | Theoretically infinite text processing |
| Ecosystem & Polish | Highly optimized for mobile (NPU support) | Requires more DIY setup for mobile |

## The Catch: Why stick with Layla for now?
While RWKV is architecturally superior for edge devices like phones, Layla has the advantage of a highly polished mobile ecosystem. Layla is explicitly designed to tap into your phone's hardware accelerators (like Snapdragon NPUs) and supports popular formats natively. If you switch entirely to RWKV, you lose the plug-and-play mobile UI convenience and will have to manage your inference using custom scripts or distinct local architectures. [3, 9, 11] 
Are you looking to fine-tune (LoRA) an RWKV model on your own computer, or are you trying to find a way to get RWKV models running directly on your mobile device?

[3] [https://www.layla-network.ai](https://www.layla-network.ai/features)

## user @ 2026-09-24T15:44:16.818Z

Base directory for this skill: /home/user/agent-factory/.claude/skills/rwkv

# RWKV - Receptance Weighted Key Value

## Quick start

RWKV (RwaKuv) combines Transformer parallelization (training) with RNN efficiency (inference).

**Installation**:
```bash
# Install PyTorch
pip install torch --upgrade --extra-index-url https://download.pytorch.org/whl/cu121

# Install dependencies
pip install pytorch-lightning==1.9.5 deepspeed wandb ninja --upgrade

# Install RWKV
pip install rwkv
```

**Basic usage** (GPT mode + RNN mode):
```python
import os
from rwkv.model import RWKV

os.environ["RWKV_JIT_ON"] = '1'
os.environ["RWKV_CUDA_ON"] = '1'  # Use CUDA kernel for speed

# Load model
model = RWKV(
    model='/path/to/RWKV-4-Pile-1B5-20220903-8040',
    strategy='cuda fp16'
)

# GPT mode (parallel processing)
out, state = model.forward([187, 510, 1563, 310, 247], None)
print(out.detach().cpu().numpy())  # Logits

# RNN mode (sequential processing, same result)
out, state = model.forward([187, 510], None)  # First 2 tokens
out, state = model.forward([1563], state)      # Next token
out, state = model.forward([310, 247], state)  # Last tokens
print(out.detach().cpu().numpy())  # Same logits as above!
```

## Common workflows

### Workflow 1: Text generation (streaming)

**Efficient token-by-token generation**:
```python
from rwkv.model import RWKV
from rwkv.utils import PIPELINE

model = RWKV(model='<opaque-redacted>', strategy='cuda fp16')
pipeline = PIPELINE(model, "20B_tokenizer.json")

# Initial prompt
prompt = "The future of AI is"
state = None

# Generate token by token
for token in prompt:
    out, state = pipeline.model.forward(pipeline.encode(token), state)

# Continue generation
for _ in range(100):
    out, state = pipeline.model.forward(None, state)
    token = <redacted>
    print(pipeline.decode(token), end='', flush=True)
```

**Key advantage**: Constant memory per token (no growing KV cache)

### Workflow 2: Long context processing (infinite context)

**Process million-token sequences**:
```python
model = RWKV(model='RWKV-4-Pile-14B', strategy='cuda fp16')

# Process very long document
state = None
long_document = load_document()  # e.g., 1M tokens

# Stream through entire document
for chunk in chunks(long_document, chunk_size=1024):
    out, state = model.forward(chunk, state)

# State now contains information from entire 1M token document
# Memory usage: O(1) (constant, not O(n)!)
```

### Workflow 3: Fine-tuning RWKV

**Standard fine-tuning workflow**:
```python
# Training script
import pytorch_lightning as pl
from rwkv.model import RWKV
from rwkv.trainer import RWKVTrainer

# Configure model
config = {
    'n_layer': 24,
    'n_embd': 1024,
    'vocab_size': 50277,
    'ctx_len': 1024
}

# Setup trainer
trainer = pl.Trainer(
    accelerator='gpu',
    devices=8,
    precision='bf16',
    strategy='deepspeed_stage_2',
    max_epochs=1
)

# Train
model = RWKV(config)
trainer.fit(model, train_dataloader)
```

### Workflow 4: RWKV vs Transformer comparison

**Memory comparison** (1M token sequence):
```python
# Transformer (GPT)
# Memory: O(n²) for attention
# KV cache: 1M × hidden_dim × n_layers × 2 (keys + values)
# Example: 1M × 4096 × 24 × 2 = ~400GB (impractical!)

# RWKV
# Memory: O(1) per token
# State: hidden_dim × n_layers = 4096 × 24 = ~400KB
# 1,000,000× more efficient!
```

**Speed comparison** (inference):
```python
# Transformer: O(n) per token (quadratic overall)
# First token: 1 computation
# Second token: 2 computations
# ...
# 1000th token: 1000 computations

# RWKV: O(1) per token (linear overall)
# Every token: 1 computation
# 1000th token: 1 computation (same as first!)
```

## When to use vs alternatives

**Use RWKV when**:
- Need very long context (100K+ tokens)
- Want constant memory usage
- Building streaming applications
- Need RNN efficiency with Transformer performance
- Memory-constrained deployment

**Key advantages**:
- **Linear time**: O(n) vs O(n²) for Transformers
- **No KV cache**: Const

## assistant @ 2026-09-24T15:44:51.785Z

The limit you're hitting is a setting, not Laya's architecture. So I'd train Laya with a long window first and test RWKV beside it, rather than switch.

**Gemini compared the wrong "Laya".** It described Layla AI, a phone chat app that runs Llama models. Our Laya is a 421M decision model: a ModernBERT encoder plus a small head that scores answer options. Most of Gemini's points (phone RAM, NPUs, chat speed) don't apply here.

**Laya's context can grow.** I just read its encoder config: it's built for 8,192 tokens, and two of every three layers use a cheap 128-token local window. Laya was only trained at 1,024. When we train it, we can train at 4,000 to 8,000 tokens on the 3090. That covers whole findings and most bug packs, with no new model.

**RWKV's real advantage** is unlimited context at constant memory. It has two costs for this job:
- It's a text generator, not a decision model. We'd build and train the decision head from scratch, which needs far more data than retraining Laya. Laya already knows the question-and-options format.
- It squeezes everything it read into a fixed-size state. So it recalls exact details from far back, like a line number or a file name, less reliably than attention does. Our decisions often hinge on those details.

**My recommendation:**
1. **Now:** train Laya at an 8k window. It's quick and keeps Laya.
2. **In parallel:** an RWKV test. Put the same decision head on a small RWKV-7 base (0.4B or 1.5B). Train it on the same OpenJev labels, then score both models on the same held-out tests plus a new long-input test. The numbers pick the backbone.
3. **MoJev**, the 0.8B model under test on your PC, already reads up to 16,000 tokens. On the GPU it may be a third option; its fit test will tell.

**GPU:** queued as you said. I'll train once all build lanes are done, and the server will be offline only a few minutes.

**One question while you're here:** go with Laya-8k now plus the RWKV test in parallel, or RWKV only?

**Short answer: yes to both.** simple-jev gives us a strong model-based Jev now, on the Qwen 27B you already run. Its method also works on RWKV. So I suggest: Qwen 27B teaches, Laya and a small RWKV learn from it, and our J2 test picks the winner.

**simple-jev**
- It's open (Apache-2.0) and uses the same API as our Jev tools. It turns any open language model into a Jev. It reads the model's probabilities for the answer words instead of generating text.
- It already supports Qwen3.8-27B, the model on your 3090, with up to 32k tokens per question. That solves the context problem without any training.
- The catch: its server wants to load its own copy of the model, and your 3090 is full. So I'd point its prompt rules at the vLLM that's already running. I tested this just now. Your vLLM returns the answer probabilities we need: about 4 s on a cold start, under a second after that. No downtime.

**RWKV: you're right on three points**
- It's cheap to run, even on a CPU.
- It handles many tasks in parallel. Each task keeps a small fixed-size memory that doesn't grow with the input.
- It reads long inputs in linear time.
- One nuance: our inputs are 1,000 to 3,000 tokens. At that size, training cost depends mostly on model size, not architecture. RWKV's training advantage grows with input length.
- Training on more scenarios mainly costs teacher labels. A local Qwen teacher makes those free, and your repo text stays on your PC.

**Plan**
1. **Teacher:** Qwen 27B on your vLLM. First I test it with J2 (same samples and scoring as before), against Laya at 0.17 and Haiku at 0.60.
2. **Students:** Laya (FT1 is building its trainer) and a small RWKV-7. Both train on the same labels in the GPU window.
3. **Winner:** J2 picks the everyday Jev. If RWKV wins, it becomes the main student.

1. OK to go with this plan?
2. Rule 3 makes OmniRoute the only model gateway for the product. Can the dev tools call your vLLM directly, or only through OmniRoute? I'll first check whether OmniRoute passes the answer probabilities through. If it does, this question goes away.

## assistant @ 2026-09-24T16:00:03.868Z

**Agreed: train RWKV for long inputs, and keep Laya for short, fast ones.** That's the split your source describes, and it fits our work. Many of our decisions need whole reports, files or diffs, far beyond Laya's 1,000 tokens.

**One caveat that shapes the training**
- RWKV's memory stays flat, but it doesn't remember everything. It squeezes the whole input into a fixed state of a few MB, so details from far back can fade.
- Two fixes. First, put the question before the long input, so the model knows what to keep while it reads. Second, train it on long inputs with our real questions, so it learns what to keep.
- Long inputs are also where RWKV's cheap training pays off. So, as you said, training it only on short findings would waste its strength.

**Updated plan**
1. **Teacher: Qwen 27B on your PC.** It reads up to 32k tokens per question, so it can label short and long inputs. I just tested OmniRoute: it passes the answer probabilities through. So this stays within rule 3, and my routing question is gone.

## assistant @ 2026-09-24T18:41:43.446Z

**No. The yes/no limit is not real. It comes from simple-jev's prompt, not from RWKV.**

I tested it on your PC (tokenizer only, on the CPU, so vLLM was not touched):
- simple-jev ends its yes/no answer prefix with a space: `{"answer": `.
- RWKV's tokenizer joins that space to the digit, so " 1" becomes one token.
- simple-jev's safety check sees the label change shape and refuses the question.
- Without the trailing space (`{"answer":`), all nine labels, 1 to 9, are single, stable tokens.

So the model itself was never tested on yes/no. The G0 probe will run the yes/no tests too, with that one-line prompt change, marked as a change from simple-jev's stock prompt. Training was never affected, because we choose the prompt format there. I logged this "false limit" pattern (AF-AP-195) and added it to the anti-hollow-green skill so it doesn't happen again.

## assistant @ 2026-09-24T20:53:39.761Z

VERIFY-GW1 says NOT-READY, and it's right. It found two real ways the window could leave your model server down:
- **F-1:** a stop signal that arrives while the script is restarting vLLM ends the script before the restart is confirmed. It then reports success (exit 130) even when vLLM never came back.
- **F-2:** the GPU memory check (`nvidia-smi`) has no time limit. If it hangs, vLLM stays stopped indefinitely, and a stop signal can't get through.
